Effective Dynamics for the Bose Polaron in the Large-Volume Mean-Field Limit
Jonas Lampart, Peter Pickl, Siegfried Spruck

Abstract




Abstract

We consider the dynamics of the Bose polaron system, a dense quantum gas consisting of
N
bosons evolving in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in the presence of an impurity particle. The system is studied in the mean-field scaling with initially high density
\rho
and large volume
\Lambda
of the gas. In the initial state, almost all bosons are in the Bose-Einstein condensate, with a few excitations. We derive from the microscopic dynamics, in the joint limit of large densities and volumes, with the constraint
\Lambda^{3}\ll\rho
, the effective description by the translation-invariant Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian, which couples the quantum field of excitations linearly to the impurity particle.

1
Introduction

Understanding the emergence of effective theories in quantum many-body systems from their microscopic counterparts is a central challenge in mathematical physics.
In this work, we study an interacting system consisting of a Bose gas and a single impurity particle.
This impurity, often referred to as a tracer particle, is commonly used in experiments to probe various properties of the gas, such as its spatial density distribution
[
38
]
and superfluid behavior
[
23
]
. Beyond these, it has found numerous additional experimental applications
[
43
]
.

In this work, we assume that the bosonic part of the system exhibits Bose-Einstein condensation, where a macroscopic number of bosons occupy the same quantum state, called the condensate. Particles outside the condensate are referred to as excitations, and their number fluctuates due to the interacting nature of the system. These excitations were first modeled by Bogoliubov
[
6
]
using a quantum field theory generated by a quadratic Hamiltonian, now known as the Bogoliubov Hamiltonian. The validity of the Bogoliubov approximation has been studied extensively in the mathematical literature. Rigorous results for the dynamics can be found in
[
20
,
21
,
27
,
3
,
32
,
29
,
35
,
12
,
5
]
and for the static case in
[
39
,
19
,
28
,
42
,
15
,
4
,
2
,
33
,
9
,
16
,
17
,
8
,
7
,
10
]
.

When an impurity is introduced into the system, it interacts with the surrounding bosonic gas. Such impurity–boson interactions can be effectively described by the Bogoliubov–Fröhlich Hamiltonian, which linearly couples the impurity—via creation and annihilation operators—to a Bogoliubov-type field of excitations
[
22
,
23
]
.
The rigorous derivation of the Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian has been established on a unit volume with periodic boundary conditions.
In the static case, Myśliwy and Seiringer derived the effective Hamiltonian in the mean-field regime
[
30
]
, which Lampart and Triay extended to the dilute regime (the Gross-Pitaevskii scaling)
[
26
]
. These results also imply convergence of the dynamics in an appropriate sense, as shown in
[
26
, Corollary 1.6]
.
A direct result on the mean-field dynamics with weaker assumptions on the potentials and an explicit convergence rate was obtained by Lampart and Pickl in
[
25
]
.
In a different scaling regime involving a heavy impurity in a Bose gas, other results yield effective dynamics modeling the tracer as a classical particle
[
18
,
13
]
.

In this article, we consider, as in
[
25
]
, a dense regime, meaning that the interaction range of a typical particle overlaps with many others.
The main novelty lies in removing the periodic boundary conditions, which results in a non-constant condensate evolving in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
We assume that the condensate initially varies on the large length scale
\Lambda^{1/3}\gg 1
, which defines the volume
\Lambda
of the gas, while both, the impurity–boson and boson–boson interaction ranges, are of order one. Consequently, the condensate appears nearly constant on all interaction length scales.
These changes introduce significant new mathematical challenges and bring us closer to a physically realistic model.

In this setting, we prove
L^{2}
-norm convergence of the full
N
-body wave function
\psi_{t}^{\Lambda}
, evolving under the microscopic Hamiltonian
H_{\rho}
(
1.2
), to the solution generated by the Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}
. The convergence holds in the joint limit of large densities
\rho=\frac{N}{\Lambda}
and volumes
\Lambda
, assuming the relation
\rho^{\alpha}=\Lambda
,
0<\alpha<1/3
, on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Moreover, we provide an explicit convergence rate in
Theorem

2.12
.
The effective Hamiltonian
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}
still depends on
\Lambda
through condensate contributions and the Bogoliubov Hamiltonian, which creates excitations on the whole condensate volume.
After extracting a divergent number of excitations from
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}
, we show that its infinite-volume limit exists. The resulting limiting Hamiltonian
H_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
provides a translation-invariant effective description of the system, independent of the scaling parameters
\rho
and
\Lambda
, and admits the explicit Bogoliubov dispersion relation (see (
1.4
)). A precise statement on the result is given in
Theorem

2.2
.

A rigorous proof of the validity of the Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian is particularly relevant in light of its widespread use in the study of the Bose polaron, where the Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian serves as the underlying model for capturing quasiparticle behavior (see
[
23
]
for an overview). Notably, recent mathematical results have demonstrated the existence of a stable quasiparticle for the translation-invariant Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian
[
24
]
.

1.1
Definition of the Model

Microscopic Hamiltonian.

We study the dynamics of a quantum gas consisting of
N
bosons evolving in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in the presence of an impurity particle.
We assume that the Bose gas occupies a large initial volume
\Lambda\geq 1
and has high initial density
\rho=\frac{N}{\Lambda}
. Moreover, we impose the scaling relation
\rho^{\alpha}=\Lambda
,
\alpha\geq 0
. The case
\alpha=0
is the usual mean-field regime, whereas
\alpha=\infty
corresponds to the thermodynamic limit. The system’s microscopic dynamics is governed by the Schrödinger equation

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=H_{\rho}\psi_{t}^{\Lambda}\,,\quad\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}=\psi^{\Lambda}

(1.1)

with the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\rho}=-\frac{\Delta_{x}}{2m}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Delta_{y_{i}}}{2}+\frac{1}{\rho}\sum\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq N}V(y_{i}-y_{j})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}W(x-y_{i})

(1.2)

acting on the Hilbert space

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x})\otimes L^{2}_{\text{sym}}(\mathbb{R}^{3N}_{y})\,.

Here,
x
denotes the position of the impurity,
y_{i}
the positions of the bosons,
m
the impurity’s mass in units of masses of the gas particles, and
\Delta
the Laplace operator.
The interactions are weak of mean-field type with both the boson-boson interaction potential
V\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathbb{R}})
and boson-impurity interaction potential
W\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathbb{R}})
even and rapidly decreasing (see
2.4
for a precise definition). The scaling factor
\frac{1}{\rho}
of the potential
V
is chosen as a mean-field scaling and the
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}
scaling for
W
ensures that the impurity-excitation interaction remains of
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
(see Section
4.2
for details). The Hamiltonian (
1.2
) in the same scaling, without tracer particle, was first introduced in
[
15
]
and later studied in
[
14
,
35
]
.

Effective Hamiltonian.

We decompose the system’s dynamics into contributions from the condensate and its excitations, i.e., particles outside the condensate. The goal is to describe the excitation dynamics through an effective theory. This is achieved via the Bogoliubov approximation, which leads to the Bogoliubov Hamiltonian
[
6
]
.
Including the tracer particle and passing to the infinite-volume limit then leads to the translation-invariant Bogoliubov–Fröhlich Hamiltonian
H_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
. This Hamiltonian provides an effective description of the coupled dynamics of the tracer and collective excitations, known as phonons. In Fourier representation for the phonons, it takes the form

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\infty}^{\rm BF}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(\omega)-\frac{\Delta_{x}}{2m}+a\left(\sqrt{\tfrac{p^{2}}{2\omega}}\widehat{W}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}px}\right)+a^{*}\left(\sqrt{\tfrac{p^{2}}{2\omega}}\widehat{W}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}px}\right)\,,

(1.3)

acting on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{x},{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})))
, where the symmetric Fock space
{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))
is used to describe excitations.
The Bogoliubov dispersion relation is given by

\displaystyle\omega(p)=\sqrt{\tfrac{p^{4}}{4}+p^{2}\widehat{V}(p)(2\pi)^{3/2}}\,,

(1.4)

where we assumed that
\widehat{V}\geq 0
. By the commutator theorem
[
36
, Theorem X.37]
, it is readily verified that
\widehat{H}_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
is essentially self-adjoint on every core of
-\frac{\Delta_{x}}{2m}+{\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(p^{2}+1)+1
.
This model describes an impurity linearly coupled to a freely evolving field of phonons, in a homogeneous condensate with density equal to one
[
22
]
.

The translation-invariant and time-independent Hamiltonian
H_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
can then be fiber-decomposed in the total momentum of the system as in
[
24
]
, to obtain an effective dispersion relation for the impurity particle. This provides direct information about its quasiparticle behavior.

1.2
Mean-Field Description of the Condensate

In the mean-field regime, the dynamics of the condensate wave function is effectively described by the Hartree equation:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=h_{t}^{\Lambda}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\,,\quad\varphi_{t=0}^{\Lambda}=\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}

(1.5)

with

\displaystyle h_{t}^{\Lambda}=h[\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta}{2}+V\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}\,,

(1.6)

where the convolution term
V\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(y)=\int V(x-y)|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)dx
accounts for the mean-field interaction between bosons. The constant
\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
can be freely chosen, as it only affects the global phase of
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
. We choose the normalization
\|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{2}=\Lambda^{1/2}
, and, following the convention of
[
27
]
, set

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}:=\frac{1}{2}{\left\langle\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}},V\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right\rangle}\,.

(1.7)

For initial data
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
, standard arguments using Duhamel’s formula yield a unique global solution
\varphi^{\Lambda}\in C^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}},H^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\big)
of the Hartree equation, which we refer to as the condensate wave function.
The interaction between the impurity and the condensate is not included in (
1.6
), as it is subleading in the regime of large density
\rho
. The condensate, consisting of
\mathcal{O}(N)
bosons, thus evolves independently of the single impurity.
The rigorous justification of the Hartree description in the setting considered here without the impurity is given in
[
14
,
35
]
.

1.3
Excitation Dynamics

Excitation Representation.

To effectively describe excitations out of the condensate, we use the excitation representation
[
28
]
. In this framework, a given
N
-body wave function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\otimes_{s}N}
is decomposed into a component in the direction of the condensate
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
and a component orthogonal to it.
To formalize this approach and the special role of the condensate, we introduce the orthogonal projection
P_{t}^{\Lambda}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
onto the condensate via

\displaystyle P_{t}^{\Lambda}\psi:=\left|\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right|\psi:=\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}{\left\langle\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}},\psi\right\rangle}\,.

(1.8)

The projection onto excitations is given by
Q_{t}^{\Lambda}=1-P_{t}^{\Lambda}
.
Any
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\otimes_{s}N}
can be decomposed into

\psi=(P_{t}^{\Lambda}+Q_{t}^{\Lambda})^{\otimes N}\psi=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\left(\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right)^{\otimes N-k}\otimes_{s}(\chi_{t}^{\Lambda})^{(k)}

with unique
(\chi_{t}^{\Lambda})^{(k)}\in\big(\{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\}^{\perp}\big)^{\otimes_{s}k}
.
To analyze excitations out of the condensate, we define the excitation map
U_{t}^{\Lambda}:=U(\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}):L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\otimes_{s}N}\to\mathscr{F}_{+,t}^{\Lambda}
by

\displaystyle U(\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda})\psi=\bigoplus\limits^{N}_{k=0}(\chi_{t}^{\Lambda})^{(k)}\,,

(1.9)

which acts on
N
-particle wave functions and maps them into the excitation space
\mathscr{F}_{+,t}^{\Lambda}:=\bigoplus\limits^{\infty}_{k=0}\big(\{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\}^{\perp}\big)^{\otimes_{s}k}
.
Note that
U_{t}^{\Lambda}
is an isometry.
We can now introduce the excitation Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\rho}^{\rm{ex}}(t)=U_{t}^{\Lambda}H_{\rho}(U_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}+{\mathrm{i}}(\partial_{t}U_{t}^{\Lambda})(U_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}\,,

(1.10)

which describes the microscopic dynamics in the excitation space, by satisfying

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}^{\Lambda}=H_{\rho}\psi_{t}^{\Lambda}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\mathrm{i}}\partial_{t}U_{t}^{\Lambda}\psi_{t}^{\Lambda}=H_{\rho}^{\rm ex}(t)U_{t}^{\Lambda}\psi_{t}^{\Lambda}\,.

(1.11)

Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian.

To motivate the definition of
H_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
, we first introduce the volume-dependent Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian
H_{\Lambda}^{\rm BF}(t)
and show that we can make sense of its infinite-volume limit, giving rise to
H_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
.

We start by deriving the Bogoliubov Hamiltonian from
H_{\rho}^{\rm{ex}}(t)
by expressing it in second quantization with a basis that includes the condensate wave function. Replacing all creation and annihilation operators of the condensate by
\sqrt{N}
, and neglecting terms that are small when the number of excitations is negligible compared to the total particle number
N
then yields the Bogoliubov Hamiltonian.
Applying this procedure to all parts of the excitation Hamiltonian that are independent of the tracer leads to the effective Bogoliubov Hamiltonian, describing the excitation dynamics without an impurity. It acts on the Fock space
{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))
and has the form

\displaystyle H^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda}(t)={\mathrm{d}}\Gamma\big(h_{t}^{\Lambda}+K_{1}^{\Lambda}(t)\big)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\big((K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)J)_{mn}a_{m}^{*}a_{n}^{*}+\mathrm{h.c.}\big)\,,

(1.12)

where
h_{t}^{\Lambda}
is the mean-field Hamiltonian from the Hartree equation (
1.5
),
\mathrm{h.c.}
denotes the Hermitian conjugate and the operators
K_{1}^{\Lambda}(t)
and
K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)
are defined by

\displaystyle K_{1}^{\Lambda}(t)

\displaystyle:=Q_{t}^{\Lambda}\tilde{K}_{1}^{\Lambda}(t)Q_{t}^{\Lambda}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{1}^{\Lambda}(t):L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,

(1.13)

\displaystyle K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)

\displaystyle:=Q_{t}^{\Lambda}\tilde{K}_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)JQ_{t}^{\Lambda}J^{*}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{2}^{\Lambda}(t):(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))^{*}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,

(1.14)

with

\displaystyle[\tilde{K}_{1}^{\Lambda}(t)\psi](x)

\displaystyle:=\int[\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}(x)V(x-y)(\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}(y)]\psi(y)dy\,,

(1.15)

\displaystyle[\tilde{K}_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)J\psi](x)

\displaystyle:=\int[\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}(x)\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}(y)V(x-y)]\psi^{*}(y)dy\,,

(1.16)

where
J
maps
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
into its dual with
J\psi={\left\langle\psi,\,.\,\right\rangle}_{L^{2}}
.
Note that
K_{1}^{\Lambda}\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}},\text{HS}(L^{2}))
,
K_{2}^{\Lambda}\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}},\text{HS}((L^{2})^{*},L^{2}))
,
K_{1}^{\Lambda}(t)
self-adjoint and we have
K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)^{*}=JK_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)J
.
The operators
a_{m}^{*}
and
a_{m}
denote creation and annihilation operators of
u_{m}
, where
\{u_{m}(t)\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a time-dependent orthonormal basis of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
including the normalized condensate
u_{0}(t):=\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}/\Lambda^{1/2}
.
We set
(K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)J)_{mn}={\left\langle u_{m},K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)Ju_{n}\right\rangle}
.
To prove the validity of the Bogoliubov dynamics, it is essential to control the number of excitations (see Section
3
).

Repeating the procedure above for the missing tracer contributions, we obtain the finite-volume Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian acting on the space
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\otimes{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})))
, effectively modeling the dynamics of the full system:

\displaystyle H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)=-\frac{\Delta_{x}}{2m}+a(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}W_{x}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda})+a^{*}(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}W_{x}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda})+H^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda}(t)\,,

(1.17)

where
W_{x}(y):=W(x-y)
.
This model describes an impurity linearly coupled to the field of excitations, while the excitation-excitation interaction is quadratic in the creation and annihilation operators.

Note that in
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}
the mean-field contribution of the condensate–impurity interaction,
\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
, is omitted. In fact, given that the condensate is sufficiently flat near the impurity at the initial time, it can be approximated by a constant, so that
\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)\sim\rho^{1/2}W\ast 1
. Thus, it can be neglected in the dynamics, as shown in Section
4
. This omission is crucial: a non-constant mean-field contribution would dominate the impurity’s dynamics, masking its interaction with the excitations and potentially causing it to escape the Bose gas on short timescales (see
4.1
).

Remark 1.1

(Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Dynamics)
.

The differential equation

{\mathrm{i}}\partial_{t}\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}={}H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}
,
\psi_{\Lambda,t=0}^{\mathrm{BF}}=\psi_{0}
has in a weak sense the unique global solution given by

\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}={}U^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t,0)\psi_{0}
,
\forall\psi_{0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2})),

where
U^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t,0)
is the unitary propagator of
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)
(see
[
27
, Theorem 8]
and
[
41
, Appendix D]
).
The dynamics generated by
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)
leaves the excitation space invariant:

\displaystyle U^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t,t_{0})\big(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(\{\varphi_{t_{0}}^{\Lambda}\}^{\perp}))\cap L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},Q({\mathcal{N}}))\big)

\displaystyle\subset L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(\{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\}^{\perp}))\,,

which can be proven analogously to
[
27
, Theorem 7]
.

Infinite-Volume Dynamics.

The effective Hamiltonian
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)
depends on the volume
\Lambda
in two ways. First, through the condensate
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
, whose initial datum varies on the scale
\Lambda^{1/3}
. Second, through
H^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda}(t)
, which creates excitations on the whole condensate volume
\Lambda
.
In order to obtain a genuine limiting dynamics, independent of the scaling parameter
\Lambda=\rho^{\alpha}
, we have to remove the
\Lambda
-dependence from the effective dynamics.
In addition the initial data may also contain a number of excitations that diverges with the volume and must be extracted (see
Section

5
for details).

Given the right initial conditions on the condensate, especially that it is flat around the origin, we can show that
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
converges locally to a phase
{\mathrm{e}}^{-it\nu}
,
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}
(see
C.4
)

To study the infinite-volume limit of
H_{\Lambda}^{\rm Bog}(t)
, we introduce Bogoliubov transformations.
We call an operator
{\mathcal{Z}}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\oplus JL^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\big)
a Bogoliubov map if

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}:=\begin{pmatrix}c&J^{*}bJ^{*}\\
b&JcJ^{*}\end{pmatrix}

(1.18)

and if it satisfies the symplectic condition
{\mathcal{Z}}S{\mathcal{Z}}^{*}={\mathcal{Z}}^{*}S{\mathcal{Z}}=S
, where
S=\text{diag}(1,-1)
. A Bogoliubov map
{\mathcal{Z}}
is called unitarily implementable if there exists a unitary
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
on the bosonic Fock space
{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2})
with

\displaystyle U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}a(f)U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{*}

\displaystyle=a(cf)+a^{*}(J^{*}bf)\,,

(1.19)

\displaystyle U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}a^{*}(f)U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{*}

\displaystyle=a(J^{*}bf)+a^{*}(cf)\,.

(1.20)

We refer to
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
as a Bogoliubov transformation, and the states
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\Omega
are called quasi-free states (for a detailed discussion of Bogoliubov transformations see
[
31
,
34
,
7
]
).

Now, to obtain a candidate for the limit of
H^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda}(t)
we look at its propagator
U_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{Bog}}:=U^{\mathrm{Bog}}_{\Lambda}(t,0)=U_{{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}}
, which is a Bogoliubov transformation (see e.g.
[
1
, Theorem 2.2]
or
[
7
, Lemma 4.8]
), where
{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\oplus JL^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{A}}^{\Lambda}(t){\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}\,,\quad{\mathcal{V}}_{0}^{\Lambda}=I\,,

(1.21)

with

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{\Lambda}(t)=\begin{pmatrix}h_{t}^{\Lambda}+K_{1}^{\Lambda}(t)&-K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)\\
K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)^{*}&-J(h_{t}^{\Lambda}+K_{1}^{\Lambda}(t))J^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1.22)

On the level of the Bogoliubov maps
{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}
, we are able to take the limit
\Lambda\to\infty
. That is, there exists a limiting Bogoliubov map
{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty}
, such that
{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\nu S}\to{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty}
in the strong sense.
For boson–boson interactions of positive type,
\widehat{V}\geq 0
, the limiting dynamics admits a diagonal form in Fourier representation:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty}}{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}t\omega}&0\\
0&Je^{-{\mathrm{i}}t\omega}J^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(1.23)

for a diagonalizing operator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\tau+\tau^{-1}&(\tau-\tau^{-1})R{\mathcal{C}}J^{*}\\
J{\mathcal{C}}R(\tau-\tau^{-1})&J(\tau+\tau^{-1})J^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(1.24)

where
\tau(p)=\sqrt{p^{2}/(2\omega(p))}
,

{\mathcal{C}}\psi=\psi^{*}
and
R\psi(p)=\psi(-p)
(see for example
[
11
]
). Although
{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty}
itself is not unitarily implementable, the diagonal evolution
e^{-{\mathrm{i}}t\omega}\oplus Je^{-{\mathrm{i}}t\omega}J^{*}
admits a unitary implementation.
We emphasize that
{\mathcal{T}}
does not satisfy Shale’s criterion
[
40
]
for unitary implementability on Fock space, since the off-diagonal term
\tau-\tau^{-1}
fails to be Hilbert–Schmidt. In fact,
{\mathcal{T}}
is not even a Bogoliubov map in the strict sense, as it is unbounded due to the infrared divergence of
\tau^{-1}
. This creates substantial technical difficulties in rigorously conjugating the many‑body dynamics on Fock space by
{\mathcal{T}}
.

Nevertheless, at a formal level one can proceed as follows: First, take the formal limit
\Lambda\to\infty
in the
H_{\Lambda}^{\rm BF}
. Then, assuming hypothetically that
{\mathcal{T}}
were unitarily implementable, one conjugates the limiting Hamiltonian by the corresponding unitary. This procedure yields a natural candidate for the infinite-volume Bogoliubov–Fröhlich Hamiltonian governing the transformed dynamics, namely
H_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
in (
1.3
) (see
Section

5
for more details). The rigorous validity of
H_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
is established in
Theorem

2.2
.

2
Main Results

We now come to the main results of this article, which establish the validity of the Bogoliubov-Fröhlich dynamics in the joint limit of large initial volumes
\Lambda=\rho^{\alpha}
and large initial densities
\rho
for
0<\alpha<1/3
.
We first present the result for the infinite-volume Hamiltonian
H^{\mathrm{BF}}_{\infty}
, followed by the corresponding statement for the finite-volume case
H^{\mathrm{BF}}_{\Lambda}(t)
.

2.1
Infinite-Volume Dynamics

To derive the infinite-volume limit, we introduce a unitary implementable approximation
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
of the limiting operator
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}
, which diagonalizes the Bogoliubov dynamics.
We then show that the transformed microscopic dynamics, with initial state
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}U_{0}^{\Lambda}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\to\psi_{0}^{\infty}
, converges to the dynamics of the infinite-volume Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian
H_{\infty}^{\rm BF}
with initial state
\psi_{0}^{\infty}
.

Applying the Bogoliubov transformation
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}
to the microscopic initial state
U_{0}^{\Lambda}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}
yields a representation in which a divergent number of excitations, scaling with the volume, can be extracted. These excitations are interpreted as arising from the Bogoliubov-transformed vacuum (see
3.4
).
After extracting this expected divergent contribution from the initial state, it is natural to take the infinite-volume limit
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}U_{0}^{\Lambda}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\to\psi_{0}^{\infty}
, which is further justified in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

The precise conditions under which
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
approximates
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}
are collected in the following condition, which will later be applied to more general maps
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}
beyond the specific choice
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}
.

Condition 2.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
be a family of unitarily implementable Bogoliubov maps,
\epsilon>0
, and
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
the initial condensate. We say
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfies
2.1
with growth rate
\epsilon
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that, for all
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{\rm op}\leq C\Lambda^{\epsilon}\,,\quad\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda})^{*}-1\|_{\rm HS}\leq C\Lambda^{1/2+\epsilon}\,,

(2.1)

and if
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}^{*}
leaves
{\mathcal{F}}(\{\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\}^{\perp})
and
Q({\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(-\Delta+1))
invariant.

Moreover, we suppose that there exists a linear operator
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}:\ran\,\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}\subset L^{2}\oplus JL^{2}\to L^{2}\oplus JL^{2}
such that
{\mathcal{J}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}{\mathcal{J}}={\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}
with
{\mathcal{J}}=\begin{pmatrix}&J^{*}\\
J&\end{pmatrix}
,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}(\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}})^{-1}
is bounded, and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}
is approximated by
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
. This is,

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}-\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}}\right){\mathcal{T}}^{-1}(\tau\oplus J\tau J^{*})F\right\|_{L^{2}\oplus JL^{2}}\to 0\,,\quad\text{as }\Lambda\to\infty

(2.2)

for all
F\in L^{2}\oplus JL^{2}
. Furthermore, we require that the commutator of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}-{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}
with translations
T_{x}
,
T_{x}f(y)=f(y-x)
, converges to zero:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{1}{(1+x^{2})^{1/2}}\Big\|\left[{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}-{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty},T_{x}\oplus JT_{x}J^{*}\right]F\Big\|_{L^{2}\oplus JL^{2}}\to 0\,,\quad\text{as }\Lambda\to\infty

(2.3)

for all
F\in L^{2}\oplus JL^{2}
.

An explicit example of a family
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
that approximates
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}
in the sense of
2.1
, with an arbitrary growth rate
0<\epsilon<1/3
, is constructed in
Appendix

D
, assuming that the initial condensate
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
is real-valued.

We are now in a position to state the main result in the infinite-volume case.

Theorem 2.2

(Infinite-Volume Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Dynamics)
.

Assume that the potentials
V
and
W
are Schwartz functions, with
\widehat{V}\geq 0
and
\widehat{V}(0)>0
. For given
0<\alpha<1/3
choose
\Lambda=\rho^{\alpha}
,
0<\epsilon<1/4\min\{1/6,\,2/\alpha(1/3-\alpha)\}
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with
n>9/4(1/\alpha-2)
.

Let
\eta\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
,
\|\eta\|_{2}=1
, and let the initial data of the Hartree equation be
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}(y)=\eta(\Lambda^{-1/3}y)
.
Assume that the condensate is flat around the origin, namely for all
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}^{3}
with
1\leq|\beta|\leq 2n-1
we have

\eta(0)=1\,,\quad\partial^{\beta}\eta(0)=0\,.

Furthermore, assume that there exists a family of unitarily implementable Bogoliubov maps
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
that approximates
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}
, defined in (
1.24
), in the sense of
2.1
with growth rate
\epsilon
.

Let
\psi_{0}^{\infty}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})))
and assume that there exist a family of states
\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},L^{2}_{\rm s}({\mathbb{R}}^{3N}))
with
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}U_{0}^{\Lambda}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\to\psi_{0}^{\infty}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))
as
\rho^{\alpha}=\Lambda\to\infty
, where
U_{t}^{\Lambda}
denotes the excitation map.

Then we have that for all times
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}\left\|{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\nu_{t}^{\Lambda}}U_{\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty}\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}^{-1}}U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}U_{{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}}^{*}U_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}tH_{\rho}}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}-{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}tH_{\infty}^{\rm BF}}\psi_{0}^{\infty}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}))}\to 0

as
\rho^{\alpha}=\Lambda\to\infty
, where we set

\nu_{t}^{\Lambda}=\int^{t}_{0}(\rho^{1/2}\int W-\mu_{s}^{\Lambda})ds\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is given by (
1.7
).
By
U_{{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}}
we denote the propagator of
H^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda}(t)
, and the diagonal Bogoliubov map
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty}\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}^{-1}
is given in (
1.23
).

Proof of
Theorem

2.2
.

This theorem follows directly from more general
Theorem

5.1
, proven later in the article, and the fact that
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}
diagonalizes the infinite-volume limit of
{\mathcal{V}}^{\Lambda}_{t}
(see (
1.23
)). Further details of the proof can be found in
Section

5
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

Note that
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}
diagonalizes the limit of
{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}
(see (
1.23
)), and that it is neither unitarily implementable nor bounded. The Bogoliubov maps
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{\Lambda}=\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty}\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}^{-1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}({\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda})^{-1}
are used as a unitarily implementable approximation of
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}
. Since, formally
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}^{-1}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
converges to the identity and
({\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda})^{-1}
to
({\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty})^{-1}
, we also have the convergence of
{\mathcal{T}}_{t}^{\Lambda}
to the time-independent
{\mathcal{T}}
. A rigorous proof of this convergence follows from the convergence of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
in
2.1
together with the convergence of
{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\Lambda}
shown in
Lemma

C.6
.

The action of
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{\Lambda}
can be interpreted in three steps: first, it removes the Bogoliubov time evolution from the dynamics; next it changes the reference state via
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
; finally, it reintroduces the Bogoliubov dynamics, but now in the infinite-volume limit, through
\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\infty}\widecheck{{\mathcal{T}}}^{-1}
.

One can generalize the assumptions on the potential and the condensate in
Theorem

2.2
. For the more general but technical conditions, see
Theorem

5.1
.

The Scaling and Comparison to the
\beta
-Scaling.

The considered scaling with
\rho^{\alpha}=\Lambda
,
\alpha>0
, introduced in
[
15
,
35
]
provides a framework for approaching the thermodynamic limit in the dense regime. The usual mean-field scaling is obtained at
\alpha=0
, while the thermodynamic limit corresponds to
\alpha=\infty
. In contrast, most of the literature focuses on the dilute regime, known as the
\beta
-scaling.
To compare with the standard
\beta
-scaling, we place the system in a box of volume
\Lambda
and rescale it to unit volume, yielding for the bosonic part of the system the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Delta_{y_{i}}}{2N^{2\beta}}+N^{3\beta-1}\sum\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq N}V\big(N^{\beta}(y_{i}-y_{j})\big)\,,\,\quad y_{i}\in[-1/2,1/2]^{3}\,,

(2.4)

with
\beta=\frac{\alpha}{3(1+\alpha)}
.
Thus, our scaling is not directly comparable to the
\beta
-scaling in the literature. While the interaction potential can be written in the same way, (
2.4
) involves a semi-classically scaled Laplacian.

2.2
Finite-Volume Dynamics

While
Theorem

2.2
assumes simplified conditions, we consider for
H^{\mathrm{BF}}_{\Lambda}(t)
in a more general framework with weaker assumptions discussed below.

2.2.1
Conditions

Conditions on the Potentials.

Both potentials may depend on
\Lambda
and
\rho
provided that all bounds below are satisfied uniformly. However, this dependence is not captured in our notation.

Assumption 2.4

(Assumptions on the Potentials)
.

We denote the boson-boson interaction potential by
V\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R})
. The potential
V
is even and for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all densities
\rho\geq 1
and all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\|\widehat{V}\|_{1}+\big\lVert\thinspace\lvert y\rvert^{k}V\,\big\rVert_{1}+\big\lVert\thinspace\lvert y\rvert V\,\big\rVert_{\infty}\leq C\,.

(2.5)

We denote the boson-impurity interaction potential by
W\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathbb{R}})
. The potential
W
is even and
\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}

\exists C>0
such that
\forall\Lambda,\rho\geq 1

\displaystyle\|W\|_{H^{2}}+\big\|\,|y|^{k}W\,\big\|_{\infty}\leq C\,.

(2.6)

We say
V
and
W
satisfy
2.4
M
for
M\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
if the assumptions above are satisfied and
\exists C>0
such that
\forall\Lambda,\rho\geq 1

\displaystyle\|W\|_{W^{M,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathbb{R}})}+\|(1+y^{2}-\Delta)^{1/4}\partial^{\beta}W\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\quad\text{for all }|\beta|\leq M\,.

(2.7)

Remark 2.5
.

•

2.4
1
for
M=1
is assumed throughout the whole article without being mentioned explicitly. Whenever additional regularity of the boson–tracer interaction potential
W
is required (i.e.,
M>1
), we refer to this explicitly by
2.4
M
. Note that we use
W\in W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{2}
for the well-posedness of the Bogoliubov-Fröhlich dynamics stated in
1.1
.

•

There exists a decreasing function
k_{0}
of
\alpha
, with
\alpha
given by
\rho=\Lambda^{\alpha}
, such that it suffices to assume (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) only for all
k\leq k_{0}
.

Conditions on the Condensate.

Different from
Theorem

2.2
we do not need to require that the condensate wave function is a rescaled function varying on the scale
{\mathcal{O}}(\Lambda^{1/3})
.
In the following, we weaken this condition by only requiring that certain
L^{p}
-norms are consistent with the scaling behavior of a rescaled condensate.

Condition 2.6

(Initial Condition of the Condensate)
.

For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
, let the condensate wave function
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.
We say
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfies
2.6
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\,\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\,}\right\|_{1}\leq C\,,\quad\|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{2}=\Lambda^{1/2}\,,

(2.8)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\nabla\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}}\|_{1}\leq C\Lambda^{-\frac{1}{3}}\,,\quad\|\nabla\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{2}\leq C\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{3}}\,,\quad\|\Delta\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{2}\leq C\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{3}}\,.

(2.9)

Next, we specify a condition that tracks the required bounds on the derivatives of the condensate.

Condition 2.7

(Initial Condition for higher Derivatives of the Condensate)
.

For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
, let the condensate
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.

i)

We say
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfies
2.7
i)
k
for a given
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
if for all
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
with
0\leq|\beta|\leq k
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\beta}\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{\infty}\leq C\Lambda^{-|\beta|/3}\,.

(2.10)

ii)

We say
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfies
2.7
ii)
k
for a given
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
if for all
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}
with
0\leq|\beta|\leq k
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\beta}\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{2}\leq C\Lambda^{-|\beta|/3+1/2}\,.

(2.11)

We say
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfies
2.7
k
for a given
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
if both
2.7
i)
k
and
2.7
ii)
k
are satisfied.

The generalized flatness condition around the origin of the condensate is given below.

Condition 2.8

(Condensate, Flat Around the Origin)
.

For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
, let the condensate
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.
We say
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfies
2.8
k,s
for a given
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and
1/3>s>0
if for all
0\leq|\beta|\leq k-1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle|\partial^{\beta}(\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}(0)-1)|\leq C\|\partial^{\beta}(\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}-1)\|_{\infty}\Lambda^{-(k-|\beta|)(1/3-s)}

(2.12)

Remark 2.9
.

i)

For
\beta=0
, the bound in (
2.12
) gives us that the condensate is approximately equal to 1 at the origin, using

\|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{\infty}\leq C
(
2.6
).
If we consider
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
as a rescaled function,

\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}(y)=\eta(\Lambda^{-1/3}y)
,
then
2.8
k,s
reduces to

\partial^{\beta}(\eta(0)-1)=0
,
\forall|\beta|\leq k-1
.
In general, the inequality (
2.12
) implies that the derivatives of
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
are much smaller close to the origin than their supremum.

ii)

The flatness of the condensate plays a key role in controlling the dynamics of the tracer particle and has two main effects. First, the interaction of the tracer with condensate particles is subleading compared to its interaction with excitations (see Section
4
for details). Second, the tracer remains inside the Bose gas over the considered timescale of
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
(see
4.1
for details).

iii)

If
2.8
k,s
is satisfied for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
then it also holds for all
\tilde{k}\leq k
.
Clearly,
2.8
k,s
cannot be satisfied for negative values of
k
.

In Appendix
B
we analyze how the listed conditions on the condensate are propagated by the Hartree evolution. In particular, if
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}(y)=\eta(\Lambda^{-1/3}y)
then one can think of
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}(y)\approx\eta_{t}(\Lambda^{-1/3}y)
for some
\eta_{t}\in H^{\infty}
.

Conditions on the Tracer Particle and Excitations.

We now specify the initial conditions on
U_{0}^{\Lambda}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}=:\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\rm BF}
for the excitation dynamics.
For this we assume that the excitation number is of order one locally and essentially of order
\Lambda
globally.
That is, only
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
many excitations effectively interact with the tracer. The control of the global excitation number is needed for the Bogoliubov approximation,
Theorem

3.5
, and the local one to prove tracer localization,
Theorem

2.14
, both are needed for the finite-volume approximation in
Theorem

2.12
.

To achieve this we assume that
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\rm BF}
is a perturbation of a quasi-free state. That is, the Bogoliubov-transformed initial state
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\rm BF}
contains at most
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
many excitations, whereas
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\rm BF}
itself may have order
\Lambda
excitations.

Condition 2.10

(Initial Conditions for the Tracer Particle and the Excitation Number)
.

For all densities
\rho\geq 1
and volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
, let
\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},\mathcal{F}(L^{2}))
.
We say
\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfies
2.10
{}_{\psi_{0}^{\Lambda},M,\epsilon}
with power
M\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
\epsilon>0
if there exist constants
C_{M},C>0
such that for all densities
\rho\geq 1
and volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
there exists a unitarily implementable Bogoliubov map
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}\oplus JL^{2})
such that
\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in Q\big((-\Delta_{x}+x^{2}+U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{2}U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}})^{M}\big)
and

\displaystyle\Big\langle\psi_{0}^{\Lambda},\left((-\Delta_{x}+x^{2})\otimes I+I\otimes U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{2}U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}\right)^{M}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\Big\rangle\leq C_{M}

(2.13)

as well as the following bounds

\displaystyle\|\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}(\tau\oplus J\tau J^{*})\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}\oplus JL^{2})}\leq C\,,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}\oplus JL^{2})}\leq C\Lambda^{\epsilon}\,,\quad\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda})^{*}-1\|_{\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}\oplus JL^{2})}\leq C\Lambda^{1/2+\epsilon}\,.

(2.15)

Remark 2.11
.

Due to elementary properties of the Bogoliubov transformation
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}
, it suffices to control only the transformed number of excitations
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}+1)U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}
in
2.10
.
Moreover,
U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}
can change the global excitation number by
\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda})^{*}-1\|_{\rm HS}^{2}+\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{\rm op}^{2}
(see
[
7
, Lemma 4.4]
). The localization of the tracer, being a local phenomenon, only requires bounds on
\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{\rm op}
, as well as the uniform bound in
\Lambda
shown in (
2.14
), which is proven later in
Theorem

2.14
. Thus, we interpret
\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda})^{*}-1\|_{\rm HS}^{2}\sim{\mathcal{O}}(\Lambda^{1+2\epsilon})
as the global excitation number, while the bounds on the operator norm ensure that the local excitation number is of
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
.

All bounds (
2.14
) and (
2.15
) hold provided
\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{\rm op}\leq C
. However, this condition does not appear to be directly compatible with the requirement in
2.1
that
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}}{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}(\tau\oplus J\tau J^{*})\to\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}}{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}(\tau\oplus J\tau J^{*})
strongly in
L^{2}\oplus JL^{2}
, since the limiting operator, for instance,
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\infty}}={\mathcal{T}}
, is unbounded.
A more careful construction of a
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfying both the operator and Hilbert–Schmidt bounds, as well as the strong convergence, is given in
Appendix

D
. Under the assumption of strong convergence, the uniform bound (
2.14
) is automatically satisfied.
The existence of a
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}
satisfying strong convergence and all bounds above is a key ingredient in our proofs.

2.2.2
Finite-Volume Approximation

We are now able to state our main result for the finite-volume
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)
.

Theorem 2.12

(Finite-Volume Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Dynamics)
.

For given
0<\alpha<1/3
and
0<s<1/3
choose
\Lambda=\rho^{\alpha}
and
n,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
large enough. Assume that the potentials
V
and
W
satisfy
2.4
n
.

i)

(Condensate conditions) Assume that for all
\rho\geq 1
the condensate
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
varies on the scale
\Lambda^{1/3}=\rho^{\alpha/3}
. That is,
2.6
with additional regularity in the derivatives of
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
, namely
2.7
i)
k+2n-1
and
2.7
m
for
m=\max\{k,2\}+2
. Furthermore, we require that
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
is flat around the origin, namely
2.8
2n,s
, and assume that there exist
\eta\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
and constants
\delta,C>0
such that
\forall\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}(\Lambda^{1/3}\,.\,)-\eta\|_{2}\leq C\Lambda^{-\delta}\,.

(2.16)

ii)

(Tracer localization and excitation number bound)
For all densities
\rho\geq 1
let
\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},H^{1}_{\rm sym}({\mathbb{R}}^{3N}))
and set
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}:=U_{0}^{\Lambda}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}
, where
U_{t}^{\Lambda}
is the excitation map from (
1.9
).

Assume that
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}
is a perturbation of a quasi-free state with localized tracer particle, namely that
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}
satisfies
2.10
2n,ϵ
with
\epsilon>0
small enough.

Let
\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(\{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\}^{\perp}))
be the solution of the effective Bogoliubov-Fröhlich dynamics with initial data
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}
(see
1.1
).
Then for all times
T\geq 0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\rho\geq 1

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}\|{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\nu_{t}^{\Lambda}}U_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}tH_{\rho}}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}-\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}\|\leq C\rho^{\frac{3(1+2\epsilon)\alpha-1}{2}}\,,

(2.17)

where
\nu_{t}^{\Lambda}=\int^{t}_{0}(\rho^{1/2}\int W-\mu_{s}^{\Lambda})ds\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
as in (
1.7
).

Remark 2.13
.

We have explicit control on the lower bounds on
n
and
k
and upper bound on
\epsilon
such that
Theorem

2.12
is valid:

\displaystyle n\geq\frac{3}{4(1/3-s)}\bigg(\frac{1}{\alpha}-2-\frac{s}{3}\bigg)\,,\quad k\geq\frac{(2n-1/2)(1/3-s)}{1/3+s}\,,

(2.18)

\displaystyle 0<\epsilon<1/4\min\{\delta,\,s,\,3/2(1/3-s),\,1/6,\,2(1/(3\alpha)-1)\}\,,

(2.19)

where
\epsilon<1/2(1/(3\alpha)-1)
ensures that (
2.17
) converges to zero.

Proof of
Theorem

2.12
.

We define
\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}
as the solution of the intermediate Bogoliubov-Fröhlich dynamics by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\partial_{t}\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\left(H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)+\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)\right)\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}\,,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\xi_{t=0}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},Q({\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(1-\Delta)))\,,

which still includes the mean tracer-condensate interaction term. The well-posedness of (
2.20
) as well as the invariance of the excitation space under this dynamics, follow by the same arguments as in
1.1
.
Then we split

\displaystyle\|{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\int^{t}_{0}(\rho^{1/2}\int W-\mu_{s}^{\Lambda})ds}I\otimes U_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}H_{\rho}t}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}-\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\int^{t}_{0}\mu_{s}^{\Lambda}ds}I\otimes U_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}H_{\rho}t}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}-\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}\|

(2.21)

\displaystyle\quad+\|{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}t\rho^{1/2}\int W}\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}-\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}\|\,.

(2.22)

The effective Hamiltonian
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)+\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
is chosen so that the leading-order contributions generated by the two distinct dynamics in the first term (
2.21
) cancel, thereby rendering it small.

In the second term (
2.22
) we extract the
\rho
-dependent mean tracer-condensate interaction term
\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}
from the dynamics by approximating it with
\rho^{1/2}\int W
, which is large but constant. For this we use the results from Section
4
, especially the localization of the tracer particle, given in
Theorem

4.2
γ
(with
\gamma=1/2(3-1/\alpha)>0
such that
\Lambda^{-\gamma}=(\Lambda^{3}/\rho)^{1/2}
).

Note that the conditions of
Theorem

3.5
κ
are satisfied for
\kappa=2\epsilon
, since
\forall 1\leq m\leq 4
:

\displaystyle{\left\langle\xi_{0}^{\Lambda},({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{m}\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}\right\rangle}={\left\langle U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}}\xi_{0}^{\Lambda},(U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}}({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{m}U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}}^{*})U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}}\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}(1+\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{*}-1\|_{\rm HS}^{2}+\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\|_{\rm op}^{2})^{m}{\left\langle U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}}\xi_{0}^{\Lambda},({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{m}U_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}}\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}(1+\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{*}-1\|_{\rm HS}^{2}+\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\|_{\rm op}^{2})^{m}\leq C_{m}\Lambda^{(1+2\epsilon)m}\,,

where we used
[
7
, Lemma 4.4]
,

\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}\oplus JL^{2})}\leq C\Lambda^{\epsilon}
,
\|{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda}({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{\Lambda})^{*}-1\|_{\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}\oplus JL^{2})}\leq C\Lambda^{1/2+\epsilon}
and
2.10
{}_{\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}},2n,\epsilon}
together with
4n\geq\frac{6}{2(1/3-s)}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-2-\frac{s}{3}\right)\geq 8\geq m
for
\alpha,s\in(0,1/3)
.
∎

2.2.3
Tracer Localization

One of the important ingredients for
Theorem

2.12
is the localization of the tracer particle, which is an interesting result on its own. For details about its derivation and interpretation we refer to Section
4.2
.

Theorem 2.14

(Tracer Localization in Position and Momentum for the Effective Dynamics)
.

Let
M\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
. Assume that the potentials
V
and
W
satisfy
2.4
max{M,2}
.

i)

(Condensate conditions) For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
let
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
be the solution of the Hartree equation (
1.5
). Assume that its initial data
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
varies on the scale
\Lambda^{1/3}
, that is,

2.6
and
2.7
4
. Furthermore, we require that
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
is flat around the origin, namely
2.8
2,s
,
0<s<1/3
, and assume that there exist
\eta\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
and constants
\delta,C>0
such that for all
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{0}(\Lambda^{1/3}\,.\,)-\eta\|_{2}\leq C\Lambda^{-\delta}\,.

(2.23)

ii)

(Tracer localization) For all densities
\rho\geq 1
and volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
let
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}\in L^{2}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{3},Q({\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(1-\Delta))\big)
. Assume that
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}
is a perturbation of a quasi-free state with localized tracer particle, namely that
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}
satisfies
2.10
M,ϵ
with power
M
and
0<\epsilon\leq 1/4\min\{\delta,\,s,\,3/2(1/3-s),\,1/6\}
.

Let
\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}
be the solution of the effective Bogoliubov-Fröhlich dynamics with initial data
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}
(see
1.1
).
Then

a)

(t\mapsto\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}})\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm loc}^{\infty}\big({\mathbb{R}},Q((-\Delta_{x})^{M})\big)\cap{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm loc}^{\infty}\big({\mathbb{R}},Q(x^{2M})\big)
.

b)

For all times
T\geq 0
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all densities
\rho\geq 1
and volumes
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}{\left\langle\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}},\big((-\Delta_{x})^{M}+x^{2M}\big)\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}\right\rangle}\leq C\,.

The proof of
Theorem

2.14
is given in Section
4.2
.

2.3
Notation

Our notation is based on
[
14
,
25
,
31
,
26
,
27
]
. Let
{\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{K}}
be
{\mathbb{C}}
–Hilbert spaces and
{\mathscr{H}}^{*}
the topological dual of
{\mathscr{H}}
.

1.

By
C
we denote a universal constant, which is independent of our scaling parameters
\Lambda
and
\rho
and whose value may change from one line to another.

2.

We denote
{\mathbb{N}}_{+}:={\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{0\}
and
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
.

3.

Let
E
be a Banach space and
p\in[1,\infty]
. We denote by
{\mathcal{L}}^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},E)
the space of strongly measurable functions
f
with
\|f\|_{{\mathcal{L}}^{p}}<\infty
and by
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},E)
the corresponding
{\mathcal{L}}^{p}
-space modulo functions vanishing almost everywhere. We set
\|\,.\,\|_{p}:=\|\,.\,\|_{L^{p}}
. By
L^{p}_{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{dn},E)
we denote the subspace of
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{dn},E)
which is symmetric under exchange of the
n
variables. We set
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}):=L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}})
.

4.

For a Banach space
E
we denote the Sobolev spaces by
W^{m,p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},E):=\{f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},E)\,|\,\partial^{\alpha}f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},E)\ for\ |\alpha|\leq m\}
,
p\in[1,\infty],m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, by
H^{m}=W^{m,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},E)
and by
H^{\infty}=\cap_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}H^{m}
. We set
H^{m}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}):=H^{m}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}})
.

5.

Let
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathscr{H}})
and let
A
be an operator on
{\mathscr{H}}
. We denote the Fourier transform of
f
and the Fourier-transformed operator associated with
A
by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(f)(k)

\displaystyle=\hat{f}(k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-ikx}f(x)dx\,,\quad k\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\,,

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}A{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\hat{A}\,.

6.

J:{\mathscr{H}}\to{\mathscr{H}}^{*}:\psi\to\langle\psi,\,.\,\rangle
denotes the canonical anti-unitary map between a Hilbert space and its dual.

7.

We denote by
\mathscr{H}\bar{\otimes}\mathscr{K}:=\text{lin}\{f\otimes g|f\in{\mathscr{H}},g\in{\mathscr{K}}\}
the algebraic tensor product and by
\mathscr{H}\otimes_{s}\mathscr{K}
the symmetric tensor product of Hilbert spaces.
For
\phi\in{\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes k}
and
\psi\in{\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes l}
we set

\displaystyle\phi_{k}\otimes_{s}\psi_{l}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k+l})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k!l!(k+l)!}}\sum_{\sigma\in P_{k+l}}\phi(y_{\sigma(1)},\dots,y_{\sigma(k)})\psi(y_{\sigma(k+1)},\dots,y_{\sigma(k+l)})\,.

8.

For
\psi\in D({\mathcal{N}}^{1/2})
and
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
we define the bosonic creation operator by
a(f)^{*}\psi=f\otimes_{s}\psi
and
a(f)
as its adjoint. For
f,g\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
we denote
A\left(f\oplus Jg\right):=a(f)+a^{*}(g)
.

9.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}({\mathscr{H}})=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes_{s}n}
the symmetric Fock space over
{\mathscr{H}}
and by
\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}({\mathscr{H}})
its truncation to at most
N
particles. For
\psi\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathscr{H}})
we denote its
n
–th component by
\psi_{n}\in{\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes_{s}n}
and define the particle number operator
{\mathcal{N}}\psi:=\sum_{n\geq 0}n\psi_{n}
on a suitable subspace of
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathscr{H}})
.

10.

For a self-adjoint operator
A
on
{\mathscr{H}}
we set
A_{j}=I\otimes...\otimes I\otimes A\otimes...\otimes I
, where
A
acts on the
j
–th space and set

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(A)\psi={}

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}A_{j}\psi_{n}\,,

on the domain of self-adjointness.

11.

Let
A\geq-\beta
, for
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, be an operator on a Hilbert space then
q_{A}
denotes the closed symmetric quadratic form associated to
A
and
Q(A)
its quadratic form domain (see
[
37
, Chapter VIII.6]
). We denote
q_{A}(\psi)={\left\langle\psi,A\psi\right\rangle}
.

12.

For
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{C}}
we denote
f_{x}(y):=f(x-y)
.

13.

For a map
A:x\mapsto A_{x}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
whose values are quadratic forms we define for suitable
\psi\in Q(A)\subset L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}({\mathscr{H}}))

\displaystyle{\left\langle\psi,A\psi\right\rangle}={}

\displaystyle\int{\left\langle\psi(x),A_{x}\psi(x)\right\rangle}dx\,,

\displaystyle Q(A)={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\psi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}({\mathscr{H}}))\Big|\,\big(x\mapsto{\left\langle\psi(x),A_{x}\psi(x)\right\rangle}\big)\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\Big\}\,.

14.

For
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},\mathcal{F}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
or similar spaces we often use the notation
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{x},\mathcal{F}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{y}))
to clearly separate the different arguments of the corresponding functions.

15.

We call a two-parameter family of operators
U(s,t)
,
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, on
{\mathscr{H}}
a unitary propagator if satisfies
U(r,s)U(s,t)=U(r,t)
,
U(t,t)=I
and
U(s,t)
is jointly strongly continuous in
s
and
t
.

16.

We denote by
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{K}})
the space of all bounded operators mapping from
{\mathscr{H}}
to
{\mathscr{K}}
, and by
\mathrm{HS}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{K}})
the space of all Hilbert-Schmidt operators. The corresponding norms are denoted by
\|\,.\,\|_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{K}})}
and
\|\,.\,\|_{\mathrm{HS}({\mathscr{H}},{\mathscr{K}})}
, respectively.

17.

We denote by
\ast
the convolution:

(f\ast g)(x)=\int f(x-y)g(y)dy

for measurable
f,g:{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\big(y\mapsto f(x-y)g(y)\big)\in L^{1}
for almost all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

18.

We denote by
{\mathcal{C}}
the complex conjugation operator,
{\mathcal{C}}\psi=\psi^{*}
, and by
R
the reflection operator,
R\psi(y)=\psi(-y)
.

3
Bogoliubov Approximation

In this section, we establish the validity of the intermediate Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)+\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
. To this end, we first isolate those contributions in the excitation Hamiltonian
H_{\rho}^{\rm ex}
that constitute error terms (see
Section

3.1
). We then proceed with the Bogoliubov approximation in Section
3.2
, where we rigorously justify the resulting intermediate Bogoliubov–Fröhlich Hamiltonian.

3.1
Determination of the Error Terms

Starting from the excitation Hamiltonian (
1.10
), we derive the intermediate Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)+\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
, which still includes the mean tracer-condensate interaction, treated in Section
4
. For this, we isolate all terms in the excitation Hamiltonian that are small when the number of excitations is small compared to the total number of particles
N
. These terms are collected into an error term
R_{N}
. Additionally, we extract the constant
-\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}
from the dynamics. The process of obtaining the effective Hamiltonian is referred to as the Bogoliubov approximation. The analysis in this section is based on results from
[
27
,
35
,
25
]
.

Unless stated otherwise, we use a time-dependent orthonormal basis
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
u_{0}(t):=\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}/\Lambda^{1/2}
. We set
V_{mnpq}={\left\langle u_{m}\otimes u_{n},V(y-y^{\prime})u_{p}\otimes u_{q}\right\rangle}
and denote by
{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)={\mathcal{N}}-a^{*}\left(\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right)a\left(\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right)
the number operator on the excitation space
\mathcal{F}\big(\{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\}^{\perp}\big)
. Observe that
{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)
and
{\mathcal{N}}
coincide on the excitation space.

Proposition 3.1

(Intermediate Bogoliubov-Fröhlich Hamiltonian)
.

For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
let
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
be the solution of the Hartree equation (
1.5
). We set
\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}:=\frac{1}{2}{\left\langle\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}},V\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right\rangle}
as in (
1.7
). Then

\displaystyle H_{\rho}^{\rm ex}(t)={}

\displaystyle H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)+\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)-\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}+R_{N}(t)

(3.1)

and

\displaystyle R_{1,N}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{d}}\Gamma\left(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}\left[V\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}+K_{1}(t)-\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}\right]Q_{t}^{\Lambda}\right)\frac{{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)}{N}

\displaystyle-\frac{({\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)+1)\sqrt{N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)}}{N}a\left(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}V\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m,n\geq 1}\rho^{-1}{V}_{mn00}a_{m}^{*}a_{n}^{*}\left(\sqrt{(N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)-1)(N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t))}-N\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m,n,p\geq 1}\Lambda{V}_{0mnp}\frac{\sqrt{N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)}}{N}a_{m}^{*}a_{n}a_{p}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}\frac{{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)}{N}+\mathrm{h.c.}\,,

(3.2)

\displaystyle R_{2,N}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{m,n,p,q\geq 1}\Lambda{V}_{mnpq}a_{m}^{*}a_{n}^{*}a_{p}a_{q}\,,

(3.3)

\displaystyle R_{3,N}={}

\displaystyle a^{*}(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}W_{x}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda})\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left(\sqrt{N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)}-\sqrt{N}\right)+\mathrm{h.c.}\,,

(3.4)

\displaystyle R_{4,N}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x){\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}(t)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}{\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}W_{x}Q_{t}^{\Lambda})\,,

(3.5)

\displaystyle R_{N}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{4}R_{i,N}\,,

(3.6)

on the truncated excitation space
\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}\big(\{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\}^{\perp}\big)
and
R_{i,N}=0
elsewhere.

Proof of
3.1
.

The error terms in (
3.1
) can be extracted by splitting the purely bosonic part of the Hamiltonian from the tracer-dependent part, and then calculating the action of the excitation map. For the purely bosonic Hamiltonian this was done in
[
27
, Appendix B and Lemma 6]
. The proof can be adapted to our setting if one replaces the potential there with
\Lambda\cdot V
and
u_{t}
with
\frac{\varphi_{t}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}
. Note that
\|\frac{\varphi_{t}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\|_{2}=1
is normalized. This gives

\displaystyle U_{t}^{\Lambda}\Big(-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Delta_{y_{i}}}{2}+\frac{1}{\rho}\sum\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq N}V(y_{i}-y_{j})\Big)(U_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}+{\mathrm{i}}\left(\partial_{t}{U}_{t}^{\Lambda}\right)(U_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}

\displaystyle=H^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda}(t)-\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}+R_{1,N}+R_{2,N}\,,

(3.7)

where
H^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda}(t)
is the Bogoliubov Hamiltonian defined in (
1.12
).

The calculation of the tracer-dependent part of the Hamiltonian can be found in
Lemma

3.2
below.
∎

Now, we follow the method displayed in
[
25
, Lemma 3.2]
to transform the tracer particle contributions to the microscopic Hamiltonian
H_{\rho}
into the excitation space.

Lemma 3.2
.

For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
let
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
be the solution of the Hartree equation (
1.5
).
Then

\displaystyle-\frac{\Delta_{x}}{2m}+\frac{1}{\rho^{1/2}}U_{t}^{\Lambda}\sum_{n=1}^{N}(W_{x})_{n}(U_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}

\displaystyle=\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)-\frac{\Delta_{x}}{2m}+A\left(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}W_{x}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\oplus JQ_{t}^{\Lambda}W_{x}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\right)+R_{3,N}+R_{4,N}\,,

where
R_{3,N}
and
R_{4,N}
are defined in
3.1
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.2
.

Our analysis is carried out for a fixed
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. We write
\sum_{n=1}^{N}(W_{x})_{n}
in its second quantized form to see that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}(W_{x})_{n}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{\infty}(W_{x})_{jk}a_{j}^{*}a_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(W_{x})_{0j}a_{0}^{*}a_{j}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(W_{x})_{j0}a_{j}^{*}a_{0}+(W_{x})_{00}a_{0}^{*}a_{0}\,.

We can transform creation and annihilation operators with the excitation map using
[
28
, Proposition 4.2]
. That is,
U_{t}^{\Lambda}=\bigoplus\limits_{k=0}^{N}Q_{t}^{\otimes k}\frac{\left(\Lambda^{-1/2}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}\right)^{N-k}}{\sqrt{(N-k)!}}
. With this we find

\displaystyle U_{t}^{\Lambda}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(W_{x})_{j0}a_{j}^{*}a_{0}(U_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(W_{x})_{j0}a_{j}^{*}\sqrt{N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\left\langle u_{j},W_{x}u_{0}\right\rangle}a^{*}(u_{j})\sqrt{N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}}=a^{*}\left(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}W_{x}\frac{\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda^{1/2}}\right)\sqrt{N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}}

and

\displaystyle U_{t}^{\Lambda}(W_{x})_{00}a_{0}^{*}a_{0}(U_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}

\displaystyle=(W_{x})_{00}(N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda})=\frac{N-{\mathcal{N}}_{+}^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)\,,

\displaystyle U_{t}^{\Lambda}\sum_{j,k=1}^{\infty}(W_{x})_{jk}a_{j}^{*}a_{k}(U_{t}^{\Lambda})^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{\infty}(W_{x})_{jk}a_{j}^{*}a_{k}={\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(Q_{t}^{\Lambda}W_{x}Q_{t}^{\Lambda})\,,

yielding the claim.
∎

3.2
Error Estimation

We aim to estimate
\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\int^{t}_{0}\mu_{s}^{\Lambda}ds}\psi^{\rm ex}_{\rho,t}-\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}\|
, where
\psi^{\rm ex}_{\rho,t}=U_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}H_{\rho}t}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}
and
\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}
solves the equation
{\mathrm{i}}\partial_{t}\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}=\left(H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)+\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)\right)\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}
.
To this end, we adapt the methods of
[
35
]
to the second quantized setting, incorporating the interaction with the tracer particle. The key tool is a bound on the excitation number operator acting on the effective dynamics
\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}
, which controls the difference between the two dynamics.
The following lemma shows that the number of excitations grows at most as the volume.

Lemma 3.3

(Excitation Number Estimate)
.

For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
, let
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
be the condensate satisfying
2.6
.
Then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and times
T\geq 0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all densities
\rho\geq 1
and volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}{\left\langle\xi_{t}^{\Lambda},I\otimes({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{n}\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}\right\rangle}\leq C{\left\langle\xi_{0}^{\Lambda},I\otimes(\Lambda+{\mathcal{N}}+1)^{n}\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}\right\rangle}\,.

(3.8)

Remark 3.4
.

The bound (
3.8
) indicates that the excitation number grows with the volume
\Lambda
.
Indeed, by applying Duhamel we see that on short timescales

{\left\langle U^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda,t}\Omega,{\mathcal{N}}_{+}U^{\rm Bog}_{\Lambda,t}\Omega\right\rangle}\sim\|K_{2}^{\Lambda}(t)\|_{\rm HS}^{2}\sim\Lambda\|V\|_{2}^{2}|\eta(0)|^{2}\,,

where we set
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}(y)=\eta(\Lambda^{-1/3}y)
and used (
1.16
) for large volumes.

The proof of
Lemma

3.3
can be found in Appendix
C.2
. The growth in
\Lambda
is determined by the estimates in
Lemma

C.1
.
The final Bogoliubov approximation is given in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.5

(Bogoliubov Approximation)
.

Assume that for all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
the condensate
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
varies on the scale
\Lambda^{1/3}
, namely that it satisfies
2.6
. For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
and densities
\rho\geq 1
let
\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},H^{1}_{\rm sym}({\mathbb{R}}^{3N}))
and
\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}:=U_{0}^{\Lambda}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},{\mathcal{F}}(L^{2}))\cap L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3},Q({\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(1-\Delta)))
, where
U_{t}^{\Lambda}
is the excitation map from (
1.9
).

If
\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in Q({\mathcal{N}}^{4})
and there exists constants
C,\kappa\geq 0
such that for all densities
\rho\geq 1
and volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
and
1\leq n\leq 4

\displaystyle{\left\langle\xi_{0}^{\Lambda},I\otimes({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{n}\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}\right\rangle}\leq C\Lambda^{n(1+\kappa)}\,,

(3.9)

then for all times
T\geq 0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all densities
\rho\geq 1
, volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
and
t\in[-T,T]

\displaystyle\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\int^{t}_{0}\mu_{s}^{\Lambda}ds}U_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}H_{\rho}t}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}-\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}\|\leq C\left(\frac{\Lambda^{3(1+\kappa)}}{\rho}\right)^{1/2}\bigg(1+\left(\frac{\Lambda^{(1+\kappa)}}{\rho}\right)^{1/2}\bigg)\,.

(3.10)

Remark 3.6
.

If we choose
\Lambda=\rho^{\alpha}
with
\alpha(1+\kappa)\leq 1
then the right-hand side of (
3.10
) can be simplified. In this case, we have that
\Lambda^{1+\kappa}/\rho\leq 1
and thus

\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\int^{t}_{0}\mu_{s}^{\Lambda}ds}U_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}H_{\rho}t}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}-\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}\|\leq C\left(\frac{\Lambda^{3(1+\kappa)}}{\rho}\right)^{1/2}\,,

which tends to zero for
\rho\to\infty
if
\alpha<1/3
and
\kappa<1/(3\alpha)-1
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.5
.

To shorten our notation we write
{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\int^{t}_{0}\mu_{s}^{\Lambda}ds}\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}=:\Phi_{t}
and
U_{t}^{\Lambda}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}H_{\rho}t}\psi_{0}^{\Lambda}=:\psi^{\rm ex}_{t}
. For the proof we use a Grönwall estimate. We start by calculating the time derivative

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\psi^{\rm ex}_{t}-\Phi_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{Im}{\left\langle\psi^{\rm ex}_{t},\left(H_{\rho}^{\rm ex}-H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)-\rho^{1/2}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)+\mu_{t}^{\Lambda}\right)\Phi_{t}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{Im}{\left\langle\psi^{\rm ex}_{t},R_{N}\Phi_{t}\right\rangle}\,.

We now use the estimate on the remainder term proven in the Appendix (see
Lemma

C.2
) to bound the right-hand side by the particle number operator acting on the effective dynamics
\Phi_{t}

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{Im}{\left\langle\psi^{\rm ex}_{t},R_{N}\Phi_{t}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq C\rho^{-1/2}\|\psi^{\rm ex}_{t}-\Phi_{t}\|\|({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\Phi_{t}\|

\displaystyle\quad+C\rho^{-1}\|\psi^{\rm ex}_{t}-\Phi_{t}\|\|({\mathcal{N}}+1)^{2}\Phi_{t}\|\,.

(3.11)

It follows with our initial condition (
3.9
) and the excitation number estimates
Lemma

3.3
that
\forall T\geq 0

\exists C>0
such that
\forall\Lambda\geq 1
and
-T\leq t\leq T

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\psi^{\rm ex}_{t}-\Phi_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi^{\rm ex}_{t}-\Phi_{t}\|\Big\{C\rho^{-1/2}{\left\langle\Phi_{0},(\Lambda+{\mathcal{N}}+1)^{3}\Phi_{0}\right\rangle}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\rho^{-1}{\left\langle\Phi_{0},(\Lambda+{\mathcal{N}}+1)^{4}\Phi_{0}\right\rangle}^{1/2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\|\psi^{\rm ex}_{t}-\Phi_{t}\|\Big\{C\rho^{-1/2}\Lambda^{3/2(1+\kappa)}+C\rho^{-1}\Lambda^{2(1+\kappa)}\Big\}\,.

The claim now follows with Grönwall’s lemma.
∎

4
Control of the Tracer-Condensate Interaction

We started with the Hamiltonian
H_{\rho}
and established the validity of
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}+\sqrt{\rho}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
for the corresponding excitation dynamics in Section
3
.
In this section, we show that the mean tracer-condensate interaction
\sqrt{\rho}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
is approximately constant, allowing us to remove it from the effective dynamics. As a result, the system is well described by
H^{\rm BF}_{\Lambda}(t)
.

4.1
Mean-Field Interaction

Overview of the Method.

Since the mean tracer-condensate interaction term
\sqrt{\rho}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
is of
{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\rho})
, it could potentially dominate the dynamics of the tracer particle and lead to the tracer leaving the Bose gas during times of order one (for details see
4.1
). However, our focus is on the interaction between the tracer and the excitations, so we choose a setting where
\sqrt{\rho}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
does not outweigh the other interactions. In fact, for the tracer to be able to interact with excitations it has to stay inside the gas cloud.
To achieve this, we show that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\rho}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)\sim\sqrt{\rho}W\ast 1\sim\mathrm{constant}\,,

(4.1)

by using that the condensate remains flat around the position of the tracer particle, specifically
|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}\sim 1
if
|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}|^{2}\sim 1
.
Note that we only need to control
|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}
within the range of interaction potential
W
around the tracer particle’s position.

The idea above is realized in two separate steps:

1. Flatness of the Condensate.

Show that the condensate remains flat around the origin, ensuring that (
4.1
) holds rigorously in this region.

2. Tracer Localization.

Show that the tracer is localized around the origin, assuming the condensate remains flat in this region.

Since we do not control the precise position of the tracer, we assume it is initially localized around a fixed point – taken to be the origin – and show that the condensate remains approximately flat around this point. Then, we demonstrate that the tracer remains localized around this fixed position over time.

Remark 4.1

(Necessity of a Flat Condensate)
.

Consider a rescaled condensate
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}(y)=\eta(\Lambda^{-1/3}y)
, which is not necessarily flat near the origin. Then the term
\sqrt{\rho}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}
contributes to the tracer dynamics. Its influence can be estimated the following way

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}{\left\langle\psi_{t},x\psi_{t}\right\rangle}=(2m)^{-1}{\left\langle\psi_{t},{\mathrm{i}}[H,-{\mathrm{i}}\nabla_{x}]\psi_{t}\right\rangle}\,.

The contribution of the additional
\sqrt{\rho}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}(x)
term in the Hamiltonian
H
can be estimated by
{\left\langle\psi_{t},(\nabla\sqrt{\rho}W\ast|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2})\psi_{t}\right\rangle}\leq C\sqrt{\rho}\Lambda^{-1/3}
. And thus for
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
times it can lead to a position change of
{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\rho}\Lambda^{-1/3})
.
To ensure the tracer remains inside the condensate of volume
{\mathcal{O}}(\Lambda)
, we obtain the constraint
\rho\ll\Lambda^{4/3}
. This conflicts with the condition
\Lambda^{3(1+\kappa)}\ll\rho
of the Bogoliubov approximation (
Theorem

3.5
). Therefore, without flatness to suppress tracer energy gain, it is not expected that the tracer remains inside the condensate on
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
timescales.

Following the strategy outlined above, we derive the following estimate, proving convergence of the intermediate Bogoliubov-Fröhlich dynamics
\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}
(see (
2.20
)) to the Bogoliubov-Fröhlich dynamics
\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}
(
1.1
) with extracted mean-field interaction of the tracer and condensate.

Theorem 4.2

(Control of the Mean Tracer-Condensate Interaction)
.

Let
\alpha>0
,
\gamma>0
,
1/3>s>0
and
n,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
with

\displaystyle n\geq\frac{1}{(1/3-s)}\left(\frac{1}{4\alpha}+\gamma-\frac{s}{4}\right)\,,\quad k\geq\frac{(2n-1/2)(1/3-s)}{1/3+s}\,.

(4.2)

Assume that the potentials
V
and
W
satisfy
2.4
n
.

i)

(Condensate conditions) Assume that for all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
the condensate
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\in H^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
varies on the scale
\Lambda^{1/3}
. That is,
2.6
with additional regularity in the derivatives of
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
, namely
2.7
i)
k+2n-1
and
2.7
m
for
m=\max\{k,2\}+2
. Furthermore, we require that
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
is flat around the origin, namely
2.8
2n,s
, and assume that
\exists\eta\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
,
\delta,C>0
such that
\forall\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}(\Lambda^{1/3}\,.\,)-\eta\|_{2}\leq C\Lambda^{-\delta}\,.

(4.3)

ii)

(Tracer Localization) For all densities
\rho\geq 1
and volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
let
\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}=\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}\in I\otimes Q({\mathrm{d}}\Gamma(1-\Delta))
. Assume that
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}
is a perturbation of a quasi-free state with localized tracer particle, namely that
\psi_{\Lambda,0}^{\mathrm{BF}}
satisfies
2.10
2n,ϵ
with
0<\epsilon\leq 1/4\min\{\delta,\,s,\,3/2(1/3-s),\,1/6\}
.

Then for all times
T\geq 0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
and densities
\rho=\Lambda^{1/\alpha}
we have

\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}\|{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}t\rho^{1/2}\int W}\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}-\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}\|\leq C\Lambda^{-\gamma}\,.

Proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

Applying Duhamel’s formula we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\|{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}t\rho^{1/2}\int W}\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}-\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}2\mathrm{Im}{\left\langle{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\tau\rho^{1/2}\int W}\xi_{\tau}^{\Lambda},\sqrt{\rho}W\ast(|\varphi_{\tau}|^{2}-1)(x)\psi_{\Lambda,\tau}^{\mathrm{BF}}\right\rangle}d\tau

\displaystyle\leq 2\int\limits_{0}^{t}\|\Theta_{\Lambda}\sqrt{\rho}W\ast(|\varphi_{t}|^{2}-1)\|_{\infty}\|\xi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|\left\|\frac{\psi_{\Lambda,\tau}^{\mathrm{BF}}}{\Theta_{\Lambda}(x)}\right\|d\tau\,,

(4.4)

where we have inserted the function
\Theta_{\Lambda}(x)=\frac{1}{1+(\Lambda^{-s}|x|)^{2n}}
,
0<s<1/3
, which localizes on a scale smaller than the
{\mathcal{O}}(\Lambda^{1/3})
scale of the condensate (see Appendix
A
).

The estimation of the localized mean-field interaction
\|\Theta_{\Lambda}\sqrt{\rho}W\ast(|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-1)\|_{\infty}
corresponds to step 1 (flatness of the condensate) and can be found in
4.3
. The estimation of the effective dynamics weighted with moments of the tracer position operator
\left\|\frac{\psi_{\Lambda,\tau}^{\mathrm{BF}}}{\Theta_{\Lambda}(x)}\right\|
corresponds to step 2 (tracer localization) and can be found in
Theorem

2.14
. We conclude

\displaystyle\|{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}t\rho^{1/2}\int W}\xi_{t}^{\Lambda}-\psi_{\Lambda,t}^{\mathrm{BF}}\|^{2}\leq C\Big\{\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-\left(\frac{1}{6}+\frac{k}{3}+ks\right)}+\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-\frac{s}{2}-2n(1/3-s)}\Big\}\,,

(4.5)

where the exponent on right-hand side of (
4.5
) is smaller than
-\gamma
due to the condition (
4.2
) on
n
and
k
.
∎

To control
\|\Theta_{\Lambda}\sqrt{\rho}W*(|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-1)\|_{\infty}
in the proof of
Theorem

4.2
, we need precise control over the localized condensate
\Theta_{\Lambda}\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
and that its flatness is preserved in time. The desired estimates on the condensate are proven in Appendix
B.2
, leading to the following bound.

Proposition 4.3

(Local Control of the Mean Tracer-Condensate Interaction)
.

Let
\alpha>0,1/3>s>0
,
n,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and
\Theta_{\Lambda}(x)=\frac{1}{1+(\Lambda^{-s}x)^{2n}}
be the localization function. For all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
, let
\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}
be the solution of the Hartree equation (
1.5
). Assume that its initial data
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
varies on the scale
\Lambda^{1/3}
. That is,
2.6
with additional regularity in the derivatives of
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
, given by
2.7
i)
k+2n-1
and
2.7
ii)
k+2
. Furthermore, we require that
\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}
is flat around the origin, namely
2.8
2n,s
.

Then for all times
T\geq 0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all volumes
\Lambda\geq 1
and densities
\rho=\Lambda^{1/\alpha}
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}\|\Theta_{\Lambda}\sqrt{\rho}W\ast(|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-1)\|_{\infty}\leq C\Big\{\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-\left(\frac{1}{6}+\frac{k}{3}+ks\right)}+\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-\frac{s}{2}-2n(1/3-s)}\Big\}\,.

(4.6)

Remark 4.4
.

By choosing
k
and
n
large enough, we can obtain an arbitrarily good convergence rate in (
4.6
).

Proof of
4.3
.

Let
T\geq 0
and
-T\leq t\leq T
.
We start the estimate by splitting
\|\Theta_{\Lambda}\sqrt{\rho}W\ast(|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-1)\|_{\infty}
into two parts

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{\Lambda}\sqrt{\rho}W\ast(|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-1)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Theta_{\Lambda}\sqrt{\rho}W\ast(|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-1)\|_{\infty}

(4.7)

\displaystyle\quad+\|\Theta_{\Lambda}\sqrt{\rho}W\ast(|\varphi_{t}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}|^{2})\|_{\infty}\,.

(4.8)

Since we assume that initially the condensate is flat around the origin (see
2.8
2n,s
), the term in (
4.7
) can be estimated straightforwardly.
We use Taylor expansion of
\Theta_{\Lambda}(x-y+y)
at
x-y
to move
\Theta_{\Lambda}
inside the convolution (see
Lemma

A.2
b) and then estimate the potential
W
with
2.4
. This yields,
\forall m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\Lambda\geq 1

\displaystyle\left|\Theta_{\Lambda}W\ast(|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}|^{2}-1)\right|(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\rho}C_{m}\|((\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda})^{*}+1)\Theta_{\Lambda}(\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}-1)\|_{\infty}+\sqrt{\rho}\Lambda^{-sm}C_{m}(\|\varphi_{0}^{\Lambda}\|_{\infty}^{2}+1)

\displaystyle\leq C_{n}\sqrt{\rho}\Lambda^{-2n\left(\frac{1}{3}-s\right)}\,,

(4.9)

where we used
